DMD

UniProt ID: P11532
Organism: Homo sapiens
Review Status: DRAFT
📝 Provide Detailed Feedback
166 Annotations 1 Core Function 131 References 1 Documents 4 Questions 2 Experiments Raw YAML

Gene Description

DMD encodes dystrophin, a cytoskeletal protein expressed as multiple products that organize links between the cytoskeleton and membrane-associated protein assemblies. Long dystrophins connect actin and intermediate-filament networks to the dystrophin-associated glycoprotein complex at the muscle sarcolemma, stabilizing the membrane during contraction and positioning associated scaffold and signaling proteins. Alternative promoters and splicing generate shorter products with distinct neural, glial and other cellular distributions. Dystrophin deficiency disrupts muscle integrity and contributes to Duchenne and Becker muscular dystrophies, with cardiac and neurological manifestations influenced by the affected products.

IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		KEEP AS NON CORE
Summary: Dystrophin products support cell–matrix adhesion in experimental contexts.
Reason: The literal mouse Dmd donor is grounded by PMID:12370193, whose human platelet observations and mouse adhesion experiments implicate short dystrophin-associated complexes. This supports a contextual adhesion contribution, without assigning an integrin receptor activity or transferring a measured activity to every human product. It is retained as non-core alongside the better established membrane–cytoskeleton linkage.

GO:0002027 regulation of heart rate
IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		KEEP AS NON CORE
Summary: Dystrophin deficiency alters cardiac rhythm control in mice.
Reason: The mouse donor is PMID:20080623. Its mdx recordings show reduced heart-rate variability and increased susceptibility to ventricular tachycardia, although basal heart rate and RR interval were not significantly altered. Retain the contextual regulatory assertion with that distinction; the experiments do not make dystrophin a pacemaker channel or establish a separate heart-rate molecular activity.

PMID:7592992
Identification and characterization of the dystrophin anchor... 		ACCEPT
Summary: Dystrophin binds the cytoplasmic tail of beta-dystroglycan.
Reason: PMID:7592992 directly tests recombinant human dystrophin fragments against a rabbit beta-dystroglycan tail translated in vitro. Human DMD residues 3054–3271 bind less strongly than a longer 3054–3446 fragment; the distal 3435–3685 fragment is negative. Retain the biologically established dystroglycan-binding activity while preserving the imported partner identity: this paper does not separately measure a full-length human DAG1 counterpart. The later human-mapped peptide complex in PMID:10932245 corroborates the contact in its fragment context.

Gamma1- and gamma2-syntrophins, two novel dystrophin-binding... 		MODIFY
Summary: Dystrophin binds the syntrophin scaffold SNTG1.
Reason: PMID:10747910 explicitly reports yeast two-hybrid and pull-down binding of human gamma syntrophins to the dystrophin C-terminal domain. The curated syntrophin family/function classification identifies a multipartner adapter that organizes membrane-associated signaling proteins, supporting scaffold protein binding. This is a partner-class refinement of the measured binding, not evidence that DMD itself catalyzes signaling or that every natural dystrophin product binds SNTG1. The current interaction query did not recover this old pair, so the positive original abstract and curator assertion remain the source-specific basis; the full original was not independently inspected.
Proposed replacements: scaffold protein binding

PMID:25910212
Widespread macromolecular interaction perturbations in human... 		KEEP AS NON CORE
Summary: Dystrophin interacts with the DNA-binding transcription factor HNF4A.
Reason: PMID:25910212 tests wild-type and mutant human proteins by binary yeast two-hybrid screening and pairwise retesting. The literal DMD–HNF4A records include a non-mutated pair and two residue-102 perturbation records for HNF4A isoform P41235-3. This product retains the nuclear-receptor DNA-binding region; curated HNF4A function independently establishes sequence-specific transcriptional regulation. The partner classification is independently supported, without asserting that dystrophin regulates transcription, binds DNA, or forms a demonstrated native nuclear complex. The source-specific binding capacity is positive, but this untargeted screen and partner identity do not establish the independent physiological pair context needed here for a more specific partner-class refinement. Retain the generic association as non-core under the project instruction; neither interaction falsity nor incompatible localization is inferred.

GO:0050998 nitric-oxide synthase binding
IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		UNDECIDED
Summary: Native direct nNOS binding is unresolved alongside positive human complex association.
Reason: PMID:27225184 establishes reciprocal co-immunoprecipitation and colocalization of dystrophin and nNOS in human atrial material, which does not isolate a binary interface. PMID:26378238 reports binding of purified dystrophin R16–17 fragments to an immobilized nNOS PDZ construct by biolayer interferometry, with an equilibrium dissociation constant of about 50 micromolar and multiphasic kinetics; the atomic interface is modeled. PMID:29790927 independently shows that human dystrophin-repeat peptides capture syntrophins from mouse muscle and that microdystrophin restores sarcolemmal nNOS only when alpha-syntrophin is present. That physiological recruitment experiment does not repeat or refute the earlier purified-fragment binding assay. The contribution of a direct contact in the native human complex remains unresolved.

PMID:21677768

Core Functions

Long dystrophins provide an integrated cytoskeleton-to-membrane anchor: the N-terminal region binds actin and keratin-19-containing filaments, while the cysteine-rich/C-terminal region engages the sarcolemmal dystrophin-associated glycoprotein complex. These complementary contacts support membrane integrity and organization of associated proteins; they are not separate core activities for each binding partner.

Molecular Function:
cytoskeletal protein-membrane anchor activity
Directly Involved In:
cytoskeleton organization protein localization to plasma membrane
Cellular Locations:
sarcolemma
In Complex:
dystrophin-associated glycoprotein complex

Suggested Questions for Experts

Q: Which natural human short dystrophin products occupy the reported mitochondrial pool, and is any pool specifically exposed to the matrix side of the inner membrane?

Q: How do native full-length DGC interfaces differ from the isolated human WW/EF-like fragment–dystroglycan peptide interface, and which conformations are product- or tissue-dependent?

Q: Which mechanisms distinguish direct dystrophin–nNOS recognition from syntrophin-mediated recruitment in the native human muscle complex?

Q: Which natural short products account for the unresolved platelet, Z-disc and neuronal differentiation observations currently represented by historical molecular masses or engineered deletions?

Suggested Experiments

Experiment: Combine accession-specific transcript/protein identification with mitochondrial fraction-purity controls, protease protection and membrane extraction to distinguish a genuine DMD product and its submitochondrial topology.

Hypothesis: Different DMD products occupy separable mitochondrial and cytoskeletal pools.

Type: Product-resolved localization and topology

Experiment: Compare matched human dystrophin R16–17 constructs and native-length proteins with nNOS PDZ and syntrophin, including folding controls, quantitative binary binding and defined multicomponent recruitment.

Hypothesis: The native dystrophin–nNOS assembly may use more than one context-dependent binding arrangement.

Type: Defined-complex reconstitution

📚 Additional Documentation

Notes

(DMD-notes.md)

DMD biological and evidence notes

Long dystrophin connects cytoskeletal networks to the sarcolemmal dystrophin-associated glycoprotein complex (DGC) and helps organize membrane-associated proteins. Human dystrophin fragment binding to a rabbit beta-dystroglycan tail and direct keratin-19 binding support complementary sides of this linkage PMID:7592992(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7592992/), PMID:16000376(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16000376/). The compact core describes this integrated anchor and organization mechanism without assigning one core to every recovered partner.

The historical human WW/EF-like fragment bound to a dystroglycan peptide is distinct from newer native complexes. Native rabbit and mouse cryo-EM structures place beta-dystroglycan near the dystrophin ZZ region and include additional cytoplasmic partners; unresolved flexible regions and fitted models limit atomic claims. Neither native ortholog structure measures all human natural products PMID:10932245(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10932245/), PMID:39663457(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39663457/), PMID:39663450(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39663450/). The human 1DXX N-terminal construct is an apo 246-residue fragment carrying C10S/C188S relative to current canonical sequence, not a full-length wild-type actin-bound complex PMID:10801490(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10801490/).

Seventeen official human sequences were reconstructed independently. Accessions and displayed product names are not ordinal equivalents. Long Dp427 products contain the N-terminal actin-binding region; Dp260, Dp140, Dp116, Dp71-related and Dp40 products have different retained regions. Sequence shortening alone establishes neither equivalence nor inactivity. Historical molecular-mass bands, splice names and engineered deletions cannot be assigned indiscriminately to current accessions. Controlled human mitochondrial fractions support a Dp71-related pool but do not resolve a matrix-facing inner-membrane location or every natural product PMID:34575126(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34575126/).

The source of a localization observation is retained when selecting a more precise term. Rat brain postsynaptic-density fractionation supports a PSD pool, not a synaptic-vesicle pool; the original journal pages are 11642–11644 PMID:1454857(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1454857/). Glial material can complicate brain PSD interpretation PMID:10545507(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10545507/). Unavailable platelet originals leave specific filopodium and binding assertions unresolved rather than disproved PMID:16803572(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16803572/).

Targeted mouse transgene experiments restore and co-isolate dystrobrevin/syntrophin assemblies with dystrophin lacking exons 71–74. This supports performed organization of the DGC, while the exact stoichiometric arrangement remains a model PMID:9214383(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9214383/). In contrast, RyR/calstabin destabilization and its pharmacological rescue demonstrate downstream calcium-handling consequences of dystrophin deficiency, not a direct DMD-catalyzed assembly reaction PMID:19198614(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19198614/), PMID:20080623(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20080623/). Cardiac sodium-current density and additional utrophin-dependent gating effects are distinguished PMID:21677768(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21677768/).

nNOS association has both direct-fragment and mediated-recruitment evidence. A purified R16–17/nNOS-PDZ BLI assay reports an equilibrium dissociation constant near 50 micromolar; kinetics are multiphasic and atomic contacts are modeled PMID:26378238(https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4705953/). Human repeat peptides capture syntrophins from mouse muscle, and microdystrophin restores nNOS at the mouse sarcolemma only in the presence of alpha-syntrophin PMID:29790927(https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6097012/). The latter study does not repeat the BLI assay and therefore does not directly invalidate it. Human atrial co-immunoprecipitation separately establishes native association PMID:27225184(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27225184/). The unresolved question is how much direct contact contributes within the native human complex. Kir-channel-tail experiments attribute their PDZ recognition to syntrophin, so they do not independently establish DMD–PDZ recognition PMID:15024025(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15024025/).

Positive generic interactions are retained as non-core under the scoped project instruction when a more informative role is unestablished. Untargeted binary hits and AP-MS associations are not treated as proof of a partner's physiological function. Independent targeted scaffold evidence justifies selected syntrophin/alpha-catulin refinements; short beta-dystrobrevin product identity remains a separate limitation PMID:21115837(https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21115837/). The source guide gives public pair identifiers, assay features, literal phylogenetic nodes, matched rule branches and positive Reactome paths.

All 166 imported annotation source objects are preserved. PAINT conclusions are evaluated on actual ancestral placement, not donor count or the presence of target self-evidence. Broad annotations are refined using supported specific terms, with pleiotropic roles retained where appropriate. No new annotation is manufactured merely to duplicate the integrated core. Eighteen unresolved assertions retain their positive evidence and exact limits.

Normal source fetching succeeded. The default Falcon research attempt returned an authentication failure, and the configured fallback returned a quota/authentication failure; neither produced a research report. Manual source research is documented here and in the evidence guide. Cached publication text, UniProt, GOA and failed provider receipts remain unmodified. A full-text cache flag is not a claim that a complete original was read: the guide records abstract-only access, independently recovered original windows and the incomplete PMID:30280653 cache separately.

2026-10-17 — response to the current-head review

The peptide-biosynthesis verdict (GO:0043043 / IDA / PMID:16000376) is corrected to REMOVE after re-reading the original Methods and Results. The expression system produces the constructs; the tested dystrophin domain performs binding and structural organization. This is a source-specific judgment from the recovered original, not from its abstract alone. Positive functions from that study remain represented.

The K19 interaction now refines to GO:0019215, intermediate filament binding. The original Figures 1–3 and 6 distinguish cellular K8/K19 filaments from purified-subunit binding. For synemin, the complete abstract establishes direct human-domain binding and colocalization, but the full original remains unavailable. GO:0019215 names an assembled filament, so that separate pair retains GO:0008092, cytoskeletal protein binding; neither a filament assay nor its absence is inferred from unseen text.

All eight Reactome cytosol annotations are retained as KEEP_AS_NON_CORE. The source guide's 16 positive participant paths still resolve to human P11532 residues 1–3685. These are broad model compartments compatible with membrane attachment, not evidence that DMD performs myosin ATP hydrolysis or extracellular ligand recognition. The R-NUL-9913739 event retains its human-dystrophin/bovine-actin distinction. The single integrated anchor core remains at the sarcolemmal DGC; its description now explicitly connects the complementary cytoskeletal and DGC-facing contacts.

Both cytoskeleton refinements retain GO:0030863, cortical cytoskeleton, in addition to the previously supported anatomical terms. Its current is_a/part_of ancestry includes GO:0005856; costamere does not have that ancestry. This preserves the cortical cytoskeletal claim while retaining the independently supported costameric distribution and the separate neuronal pool. These are refinements of existing assertions, not new annotations.

All 166 imported source objects, 17 product records, reference assessments and nine existing quotations remain unchanged. No new quotations were added. Earlier notes and the source guide remain intact; this dated appendix supersedes the earlier action categorization only where specified above.

id: P11532
gene_symbol: DMD
product_type: PROTEIN
status: DRAFT
taxon:
  id: NCBITaxon:9606
  label: Homo sapiens
description: "DMD encodes dystrophin, a cytoskeletal protein expressed as multiple products that organize links between the cytoskeleton and membrane-associated protein assemblies. Long dystrophins connect actin and intermediate-filament networks to the dystrophin-associated glycoprotein complex at the muscle sarcolemma, stabilizing the membrane during contraction and positioning associated scaffold and signaling proteins. Alternative promoters and splicing generate shorter products with distinct neural, glial and other cellular distributions. Dystrophin deficiency disrupts muscle integrity and contributes to Duchenne and Becker muscular dystrophies, with cardiac and neurological manifestations influenced by the affected products."

- id: UniProtKB:P11532
  title: Dystrophin (DMD), Homo sapiens
  findings:
  - statement: The reviewed record identifies seventeen alternative products and the cysteine-rich ZZ
      domain containing structural zinc-binding sites.
core_functions:
- description: "Long dystrophins provide an integrated cytoskeleton-to-membrane anchor: the N-terminal region binds actin and keratin-19-containing filaments, while the cysteine-rich/C-terminal region engages the sarcolemmal dystrophin-associated glycoprotein complex. These complementary contacts support membrane integrity and organization of associated proteins; they are not separate core activities for each binding partner."
  molecular_function:
    id: GO:0106006
    label: cytoskeletal protein-membrane anchor activity
  directly_involved_in:
  - id: GO:0007010
    label: cytoskeleton organization
  - id: GO:0072659
    label: protein localization to plasma membrane
  locations:
  - id: GO:0042383
    label: sarcolemma
  in_complex:
    id: GO:0016010
    label: dystrophin-associated glycoprotein complex
  supported_by:
  - reference_id: PMID:16000376
  - reference_id: PMID:7592992
  - reference_id: PMID:9356463
  - reference_id: PMID:7545544
  - reference_id: PMID:9214383
  - reference_id: PMID:39663457
  - reference_id: PMID:39663450
suggested_questions:
- question: Which natural human short dystrophin products occupy the reported mitochondrial pool, and
    is any pool specifically exposed to the matrix side of the inner membrane?
- question: How do native full-length DGC interfaces differ from the isolated human WW/EF-like fragment–dystroglycan
    peptide interface, and which conformations are product- or tissue-dependent?
- question: Which mechanisms distinguish direct dystrophin–nNOS recognition from syntrophin-mediated recruitment
    in the native human muscle complex?
- question: Which natural short products account for the unresolved platelet, Z-disc and neuronal differentiation
    observations currently represented by historical molecular masses or engineered deletions?
suggested_experiments:
- hypothesis: Different DMD products occupy separable mitochondrial and cytoskeletal pools.
  description: Combine accession-specific transcript/protein identification with mitochondrial fraction-purity
    controls, protease protection and membrane extraction to distinguish a genuine DMD product and its
    submitochondrial topology.
  experiment_type: Product-resolved localization and topology
- hypothesis: The native dystrophin–nNOS assembly may use more than one context-dependent binding arrangement.
  description: Compare matched human dystrophin R16–17 constructs and native-length proteins with nNOS
    PDZ and syntrophin, including folding controls, quantitative binary binding and defined multicomponent
    recruitment.
  experiment_type: Defined-complex reconstitution
